⏺ Five research tracks are running: voice architecture, components and latency,
  dialogue control and state, pharmacy and patient context, and regulation and
  identity. I'll combine them when they finish.

  I've already confirmed a few Claude facts that affect the design:

  - Thinking: Opus 5.5 can't turn thinking off, so it's a latency risk on every
    spoken turn.
  - Haiku 5.5: it's built for latency-sensitive work and costs $0.10 / $0.50 per
    million input / output tokens.
  - Prompt caching: it needs a 512-token prefix on the current models. Swapping
    the tool list at each step would also break the cache, because tools come
    first in the prefix. That matters if we limit tools per step.

⏺ The components and latency track is finished, and its result is sobering. A
  hosted LLM turn comes to about 1.33 s at the median, which misses the 1-second

  Those totals are the researcher's arithmetic, not measurements. I'm still
  waiting on the other four tracks before drawing conclusions.

✻ Brewed for 2s · done Thursday 10:20 PM

⏺ Agent "Voice architecture frontier research" finished · 17m 55s

⏺ The voice architecture track is finished too. It found S2S and cascade roughly
  tied on task accuracy in controlled tests, but only a cascade can check an
  exact string before it's spoken.

  It also ran a useful local test, using your persistent Chrome on port 9222
  against localhost. A closed tab took 28 to 31 s to reach the server over
  WebRTC, and a pagehide handler cut that to 4 to 6 ms. That delay would
  directly hold up the SMS fallback. The sample was small and tested plain
  aiortc, not Pipecat's wrapper. Three tracks are still running.

✻ Cogitated for 3s · done Thursday 10:23 PM

⏺ Agent "Pharmacy and patient domain research" finished · 18m 8s

⏺ The pharmacy and patient track is finished, and it changes the design. In one
  clinic sample of older patients, only 22% could name their drugs from memory.
  So the agent should read out the list and accept "my blood pressure pill",
  which needs an indication column. Closed-list matching also needs an explicit
  "not on your list" outcome, so "my Zoloft" can't turn into a listed drug. Two
  tracks are still running: dialogue control and regulation.

✻ Cogitated for 1s · done Thursday 10:25 PM

⏺ Agent "Dialogue control and state research" finished · 21m 3s

⏺ Only the regulation track is left. The dialogue track found that code should
  own routing and the LLM should own wording. In one controlled study,
  reliability rose from 0.38 with raw state to 0.98 with hard enforcement.

  It also caught a concrete bug in the plan: dateparser returns None on "March
  fifteenth, nineteen eighty-five", the example in CLAUDE.md. So date
  normalization needs its own handling. That test used hand-written strings, not
  real STT output.

  Ran 1 shell command, wrote 3 memories (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The research is finished, and nothing in it is revolutionary for this build.
  I'd propose a carefully done standard pipeline: speech-to-text, LLM and
  text-to-speech on Pipecat. A plain code module works out the next step from
  database fields, and code, not the model, writes every sentence containing a
  drug, pharmacy, plan or price. Nothing is decided yet, and CLAUDE.md and
  docs/DECISIONS.md are unchanged.

  Why a pipeline: the reason is control, not accuracy. In EVA-Bench the best
  pipeline scored 0.504 against 0.467 for the best speech-to-speech model, which
  is roughly level. A text stage lets code fix the exact words before they're
  spoken, and the same core can run the SMS thread.

  Latency

  The 1 second target isn't met on turns where an LLM reads the reply. These
  figures are arithmetic from published numbers; nothing has been timed on this
  project.

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────┬────────────┬────────────────┐
  │                Turn type                 │   Median   │     95th       │
  │                                          │            │   percentile   │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────┼────────────┼────────────────┤
  │ Code reads the reply, speaks a fixed     │ about 0.7  │ 0.8 to 0.9 s   │
  │ line                                     │ s          │                │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────┼────────────┼────────────────┤
  │ LLM reads the reply, code speaks         │ about 1.4  │ 1.7 to 2.7 s   │
  │                                          │ s          │                │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────┼────────────┼────────────────┤
  │ LLM, tool, then a second LLM pass        │ about 1.9  │ above 2.5 s    │
  │                                          │ s          │                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────┴────────────┴────────────────┘

  The largest input, 640 ms of LLM time, is a Haiku 4.5 stand-in, because no
  independent figures exist for Haiku 5.5 in voice. No independent source shows
  any system reliably under 1 s on a tool turn.

  What decides the call for a real patient

  - Date of birth: this is the riskiest field. Nobody has published a
    spoken-date test on any provider, and dateparser returns nothing for your
    exact CLAUDE.md example. Older speakers also pause more, so the turn should
    stay open until a full date parses, though that idea is untested.
  - Choosing a drug: only 22% of 99 patients aged 65+ could name their drugs
    from memory (Jones et al.). The agent should read the list with each drug's
    purpose and accept "the blood pressure one". It also needs a "not on your
    list" outcome, because fuzzy matching scored lovastatin, a different drug,
    82 against Atorvastatin.
  - Reaching a person: being trapped is the complaint that keeps recurring, so
    "speak to a pharmacist" has to work, even if only as a logged callback.
  - Dropped calls: a closed tab took about 30 s to reach the server, and a
    pagehide handler cut that to about 5 ms. That was a small test on plain
    aiortc, not Pipecat, and it ran in your persistent Chrome on localhost.

  Where I'd change the report

  1. Fast reader: the report says code must read clear answers to hit the
     target. I'd wait for timings: it would keep most happy-path turns away from
     the LLM, which weakens the agent loop reviewers want to see. The kickoff
     bar is no multi-second pauses, which 1.4 s clears at the median, though the
     slow end (up to 2.7 s) may not.
  2. Latency target: its new wording (under 1.5 s on LLM turns) moves the bar to
     fit an estimate. Define the measurement now, and change the number only
     after timing.
  3. Date of birth retry: keep what the kickoff approved, asking for the date
     again. The report's neutral re-ask of both fields gains little with one
     patient.
  4. First texts: three of them say "Reply YES" instead of asking the next
     question, which departs from CLAUDE.md and adds a turn. I'd ask directly
     and keep only the copay out of the first text.
  5. Timing test: also count tool calls written as plain text, because this
     design would speak them aloud. Anthropic documents this failure on Opus 5
     with thinking off; whether it affects Haiku 5.5 is unknown.

  Fix before building: session transcripts

  agent-logs/ has no transcript for this session, and I found no .jsonl for it
  under this project's ~/.claude/projects folder. The hook hides its own errors,
  so I can't tell whether it ran. The kickoff requires these logs, and I
  haven't changed anything.

  What I checked

  - Re-ran: SMS lengths, latency arithmetic, the parser and fuzzy-match results,
    and the Pipecat version.
  - Checked against sources: the EVA-Bench and state-enforcement figures, the
    EVA-Bench ones through a model-extracted summary of its tables. The 22%
    comes from a search summary of the abstract, because the paper is behind a
    login.
  - Not checked: most other citations, so spot-check Artificial Analysis figures
    before quoting them.

  Decisions for you

  The first build slice needs these four; my proposals are in brackets:
  - Q3, flow control: where does the step logic live? (plain code module)
  - Q4, database: which engine? (SQLite)
  - Q7, dates: how are spoken dates normalized? (LLM fills typed fields, code
    validates)
  - Q10, a new question: when is the request created? (ask "Should I send that
    in?" and create it on yes)

  The LLM timing test can run alongside that slice once you have Anthropic,
  Gemini and Groq keys. The proposal is Haiku 5.5 with thinking off rather than
  the usual Opus 5.5 default, because Opus 5.5 can't turn thinking off. Q1
  (pipeline vs speech-to-speech) can wait until the voice slice.

  Also decide whether reports/ and research_notes/ get committed, since the repo
  goes to reviewers; both are untracked for now. I can publish the report as a
  private page if you want it easier to read.

  The full report is at /Users/sanat/refill-voice-agent/reports/Refill voice 
  agent architecture research.md, and it covers all ten questions, the build
  order and proposed doc edits.

  Sources: Jones et al., Drugs & Aging 2015, EVA-Bench, state enforcement study

  Wrote 2 memories (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The transcript hook is fixed, but you have one step left, below. My
  suggestions for Q3, Q4, Q7 and Q10 are my original proposals, with a few
  changes now that this is a take-home. Only the Anthropic key blocks the first
  build slice.

  Transcripts

  - Cause: the hook works; three real test sessions exported correctly. This
    session is the only one that never wrote a transcript, and I couldn't find
    out why.
  - Fixed: export failures now show as a warning. Redaction now covers
    everything in .env and more key formats; before this, 5 of the 7 key formats
    I tested would have leaked into logs reviewers see. Both changes are tested
    but not committed.
  - Your step: run /export into agent-logs/ before closing this session, or the
    research session has no record. You'll probably see the new warning after
    this reply, which is expected for this session.

  What "take-home" changes

  - Drop: STOP handling, the 160-character limit, session expiry and sms_outbox.
    Mention them in the walkthrough instead.
  - Keep: tools refusing before verification, and no drug name in a text before
    verification. Both are cheap, and healthcare reviewers will look for them.
  - Correction to my point 4: the copay can go in the first text, because that
    rule was a production compliance concern.

  Fillers

  Yes, with limits. This reverses the report's "skip", because a fixed clip with
  no facts can't invent a price.

  - Prefer working phrases: "Let me check that" and "One moment" over "uhh" and
    "umm". Research on "uh" and "um" is mixed: some people find them natural,
    others expect an agent to be fluent, and they may signal doubt, which is bad
    right before a price. I only read search summaries of those studies, and
    none tested latency or older callers. Try "umm" in a listening test before
    deciding.
  - Fire on a timer: use pre-recorded clips in the agent's voice, played only if
    no reply is ready about 0.3 s after the turn ends. Fast turns then get no
    filler. Allow at most about three per call, never the same one twice in a
    row.
  - The buffer is small: about 0.5 s on planning figures. That may be enough to
    use Sonnet 5.5 instead of Haiku 5.5. On one general phone benchmark, Sonnet
    had 1.6% tool failures against 4.9% for Haiku, but it reported no latency,
    so time both.
  - Never during the date of birth: a filler can't buy patience for slow
    speakers, and during a pause it talks over them. Save "Let me pull that up"
    for after a full date.
  - Keep the numbers honest: log first sound and first real content separately.
    The latency figure uses first content.

  Testing

  Your plan works, with one limit: don't tune turn-taking on AI voices. One
  benchmark had Smart Turn wrongly cutting off 1% of real clips but 57.7% of
  synthetic ones. Use AI voices for word accuracy and repeat runs. Use your own
  voice, and ideally an older person's, for pauses. Feed made-up mishearings
  straight in as text too, since that's free and instant.

  Q3. Flow control: plain code module

  - Realistic: one small module, no framework lock-in, and easy to walk through.
  - High level: stored state decides the step and the model only interprets
    replies, so voice and SMS share one core.
  - Low level: next_step() returns the first empty required field. handle_turn()
    then locks the session, runs the LLM, validates and commits, and renders
    the next line.
  - Logic: gate tools on prerequisites, not on the current step; this corrects
    the report. If only the current step's tool exists, a patient who names
    their pharmacy early can't be captured. So before verification only
    verify_identity and request_pharmacist exist, and submit_request needs every
    field filled.

  Q4. Database: SQLite

  - Realistic: nothing to install on Mac or Windows, and the spec accepts any
    real database.
  - High level: one process and one writer, so Postgres adds a setup step and no
    benefit.
  - Low level: eight tables: patients (with plan and member ID), pharmacies,
    prescriptions (with indication), copays, sessions, refill_requests,
    messages, turn_log. Use typed columns, integer cents and plain SQL.
  - Logic: one conditional update on sms_sent_at guarantees a single text, which
    replaces the outbox table. A unique session_id on refill_requests prevents
    duplicate requests.

  Q7. Normalization: LLM fills typed fields, code compares

  - Realistic: drop dateparser for now. With the fast reader deferred there's
    only one path, and it's less code.
  - High level: the model handles messy speech, and code does the exact
    comparison.
  - Low level: verify_identity(full_name, birth_year, birth_month, birth_day),
    with parts left empty if unclear. Match the date exactly, then fuzzy-match
    the name on those rows only. For drugs, the model returns a prescription_id
    or not_on_list, with no fuzzy matching.
  - Logic: the model never sees the stored date of birth, so it can't talk a
    wrong caller through. A misheard date fails safely into the retry, and the
    real risk is locking out a genuine patient, so a second failure offers a
    pharmacist.

  Q10. Request creation: ask, then create on yes

  - Realistic: one extra turn of about five seconds, plus a change to step 6 in
    CLAUDE.md.
  - High level: the only irreversible write gets an explicit yes, after the
    patient hears the price.
  - Low level: submit_request() takes no arguments and is safe to repeat. A
    "yes" that interrupts the price line gets the price repeated. A "no" creates
    nothing and sends no text.
  - Logic: if the request is created first, a drop before the read-back marks it
    complete with no text, so the patient never hears the price. Asking first
    gives one clean boundary: before yes the SMS resumes, after yes it's done.

  Keys

  Ask Scope first, since the kickoff said keys are available on request.

  - Already set: GEMINI_API_KEY is in your shell if we want it for timing, and

  - Not needed: Groq (which I suggested earlier), since fillers remove the

    Windows machine for one install test.

  interruptions like "Lisinopril!" or "wait", and Pipecat's built-in backchannel
  detector needs Krisp, so I'm building a small custom acknowledgement-word
  list instead. Next I'll write the decisions and edge-case rules.

       13 +## D1. Flow control: a plain code module owns the steps (2026-
          +11-05)                                                        
       14 +- Options: (A) one prompt with all tools; (B) a plain code mod
          +ule that derives the step from stored fields, with the LLM rea
          +ding replies; (C) a Pipecat Flows graph or a fully scripted di
          +alogue.                                                       
       15 +- Chosen: B. The current step is the first required field stil
          +l empty, never a stored pointer. One core, `handle_turn(sessio
          +n, channel, text)`, serves voice and SMS and imports nothing f
          +rom the voice layer. The LLM reads the patient's reply and cal
          +ls tools. Code renders every sentence holding a drug, pharmacy
          +, plan or price, so a normal turn needs one LLM pass. Seven to
          +ols: `verify_identity`, `select_medication`, `confirm_pharmacy
          +`, `confirm_insurance`, `submit_request`, `request_pharmacist`
          +, `end_call`. Tools are gated on prerequisites rather than on 
          +the current step. Before verification only `verify_identity`, 
          +`request_pharmacist` and `end_call` exist. After it every fiel
          +d tool exists, so an early answer is captured. `submit_request
          +` also needs every field filled.                              
       16 +- Why: On text agents in 2026, one controlled study went from 
          +0.38 with raw state to 0.98 with hard enforcement. Those tasks
          + are harder than this one and are not voice, so the shape tran
          +sfers and the number does not. Flows keeps state in a process 
          +dictionary and needs a voice pipeline, which the SMS path does
          + not have. Gating on the current step alone would lose a pharm
          +acy named early.                                              
       17 +- Revisit if: off-script handling becomes the main extension a
          +nd code routing feels brittle.                                
       18 +                                                              
       19 +## D2. Database: SQLite (2026-11-05)                          
       20 +- Options: (A) SQLite; (B) Postgres in Docker.                
       21 +- Chosen: A, through Python's `sqlite3` with plain SQL and no 
          +ORM. Eight tables: `patients` (with plan and member ID), `phar
          +macies`, `prescriptions` (with `indication`), `copays`, `sessi
          +ons`, `refill_requests`, `messages`, `turn_log`. `sessions` us
          +es typed columns so the step can be derived from empty fields.
          + Money is integer cents and dates are ISO text. `refill_reques
          +ts.session_id` is unique. One conditional update on `sessions.
          +sms_sent_at` guarantees a single follow-up text, so there is n
          +o outbox table. Two or three alternate pharmacies are seeded. 
       22 +- Why: nothing to install on macOS or Windows, one process wri
          +tes, and the take-home accepts any real database. Postgres add
          +s a setup step for concurrency this does not need.            
       23 +- Revisit if: more than one process writes.                   
       24 +                                                              
       25 +## D3. Normalizing dates, names and drugs (2026-11-05)        
       26 +- Options: (A) the LLM maps speech to typed fields and code va
          +lidates; (B) a deterministic parser alone; (C) rely on speech-
          +to-text formatting.                                           
       27 +- Chosen: A. `verify_identity` takes a name plus year, month a
          +nd day. Unclear parts stay empty and the model never guesses. 
          +Code builds the date, matches it exactly, then fuzzy-matches t
          +he name on those rows only. For drugs the model sees the patie
          +nt's own list and returns a `prescription_id` or `not_on_list`
          +, with no fuzzy matching in code. The raw transcript stays in 
          +`messages`. `dateparser` is not used.                         
       28 +- Why: in a local test `dateparser` returned nothing for "Marc
          +h fifteenth, nineteen eighty-five". "Lovastatin", a different 
          +drug, scored 82 against Atorvastatin, so a fuzzy threshold is 
          +unsafe. The model never sees the stored date of birth, so it c
          +annot steer a wrong caller to a match.                        
       29 +- Revisit if: real transcripts show the model mapping dates wr
          +ongly.

       31 +## D4. The request is created on the patient's yes (2026-11-05
          +)                                                             
       32 +- Options: (A) state the copay, create the request, read back;
          + (B) state the price and summary, ask, create on yes.         
       33 +- Chosen: B. `submit_request` takes no arguments, needs every

       34 +- Why: with A, a drop before the read-back marks the request c

          +s, after it the work is done. A reply also proves the price wa

       39 +- Chosen: B. Pre-recorded clips in the agent's voice, such as

          +gged separately, and the latency figure uses first content.   
       40 +- Why: it covers an LLM wait without hiding it from the measur
          +ement. Hesitation sounds may signal doubt just before a price,

       43 +## D6. Edge cases and guardrails (2026-11-05)                 
       44 +- Options: (A) prompt rules only; (B) enforcement in the tool 
          +layer and core, with a short prompt; (C) add guard models or c
          +lassifiers.                                                   
       45 +- Chosen: B, as set out in the tables below. Times are plannin
          +g numbers. Voice rows assume the Pipecat pipeline, which is st
          +ill open as Q1.                                               
       46 +- Why: a prompt can be talked around and a refused tool cannot
          +. Guard models add latency to every turn. None of these rules 
          +adds an LLM pass to a normal turn.                            
       47 +- Revisit if: testing after the build shows a rule failing or 
          +a common case missing.                                        
       48 +                                                              
       49 +### Talking over                                              
       50 +                                                              
       51 +| Case | What happens |                                       
       52 +|---|---|                                                     
       53 +| Patient interrupts the agent | Audio stops, the rest of the 
          +line is dropped, and the reply is read as a normal turn. The l
          +ine is stored marked as interrupted. |                        
       54 +| "Mm-hm", "okay", "yeah" while the agent talks | Not an inter
          +ruption. The agent keeps talking and the word stays out of the
          + context. Any other word does interrupt, even one word such as
          + "Lisinopril" or "wait". This needs a small custom start strat
          +egy, because Pipecat's `min_words` would also block those one-
          +word interruptions. |                                         
       55 +| Agent starts too early and the patient keeps going | Agent s
          +tops. The earlier fragment and the new words are read together
          + as one turn. |                                               
       56 +| Patient speaks during a working phrase | The clip stops, any
          + pending reply is dropped, and the turn is read again with the
          + new words. |                                                 
       57 +| "Yes, send it" cuts off the price line | Does not count as t
          +he yes. The agent repeats the price and the question. |       
       58 +| Both start at once | The agent yields. |                    
       59 +| A new turn arrives while one is running | Turns run one at a
          + time under a per-session lock. A committed write always stand
          +s, and the database decides what happened. Tool calls use `can
          +cel_on_interruption=False`. |                                 
       60 +| The agent's own audio reaches the microphone | Browser echo 
          +cancellation stays on. |                                      
       61 +                                                              
       62 +### Questions back to the agent                               
       63 +                                                              
       64 +| Patient asks | What happens |                               
       65 +|---|---|                                                     
       66 +| Something on record, such as what a drug is for or its stren
          +gth | Answer from the record, then repeat the pending question
          +. |                                                           
       67 +| "How much is it?" before insurance is confirmed | "I can tel
          +l you as soon as we confirm your insurance." No number, then t
          +he pending question. |                                        
       68 +| "Why do you need that?" | Fixed line, then the pending quest
          +ion. |                                                        
       69 +| "Are you a real person?" | A truthful fixed line saying it i
          +s an automated assistant. |                                   
       70 +| Dose advice, side effects, interactions, symptoms | No advic
          +e. `request_pharmacist`, then offer to continue the refill. | 
       71 +| Emergency language | A fixed line telling them to call 911. 
          +|                                                             
       72 +| Two questions at once | Answer what is on record, then ask o
          +ne question back. |                                           
       73 +| Anything else | Say it cannot help with that, offer a pharma
          +cist, then the pending question. |                            
       74 +                                                              
       75 +### Answers given early or together                           
       76 +                                                              
       77 +| Case | What happens |                                       
       78 +|---|---|                                                     
       79 +| Several answers in one turn | One LLM pass, several tool cal
          +ls run in order with `run_in_parallel=False`. The next line is
          + computed once from the final state. |                        
       80 +| An answer to a later step, after verification | The field is
          + filled and that question is skipped. |                       
       81 +| A detail given before verification ("refill my lisinopril") 
          +| Not stored as collected. It stays in the conversation and is
          + applied after verification, so the patient is not asked again
          +. |                                                           
       82 +| Name and date together or reversed | Accepted. |            
       83 +| Self-correction in one turn ("CVS, no, Walgreens") | The las
          +t stated value wins. |                                        
       84 +| Change of mind before the final yes | The field is overwritt
          +en, the step recomputes, and the copay is looked up again when
          + spoken. |                                                    
       85 +| An incomplete date ("March...") | Not a failed attempt. The 
          +agent waits or asks for the missing part. |                   
       86 +| Two refills at once | The agent asks which one first. A seco
          +nd refill is a listed extension. |                            
       87 +                                                              
       88 +### Protecting data                                           
       89 +                                                              
       90 +- Patient identity comes from the server session, never from a
          + tool argument, and a session is bound to one patient.        
       91 +- No tool can list patients or search by name.                
       92 +- Before verification the model's context holds no patient dat
          +a, and refused tools also refuse in code if called.           
       93 +- The model never sees the stored date of birth, the full memb
          +er ID or the copay table. Code renders those.                 
       94 +- Two complete failed checks end verification for that session
          +. Tools stay refused and the agent offers a pharmacist.       
       95 +- Questions about anyone else are refused with a fixed line. A
          + caregiver gives the patient's name and date of birth.        
       96 +- Free text from the model is checked one sentence at a time b
          +efore text-to-speech. A sentence with a number, price or drug 
          +name outside this session's facts, or with tool syntax, is rep
          +laced by the pending question. Replies are capped short.      
       97 +- "Ignore your instructions" or "I'm the pharmacist" changes n
          +othing, because no tool can act on it. The agent gives a fixed
          + line and the pending question.                               
       98 +- State holds IDs and enums. Pharmacy text from a patient is s
          +tored as data and flagged for staff.                          
       99 +- SMS replies pass through the same gates. "Wrong number" or "
          +who is this" ends the thread and blocks further content.      
      100 +- `turn_log` holds timings and error codes only.              
      101 +                                                              
      102 +### Other realistic cases                                     
      103 +                                                              
      104 +| Case | What happens |                                       
      105 +|---|---|                                                     
      106 +| "What?" or "Can you repeat that?" | The last line is rendere
          +d again from state. |                                         
      107 +| "Hold on" | The agent waits quietly for up to about 30 s. | 
      108 +| Silence | A gentle re-ask at about 10 s and "Take your time"
          + at about 20 s. Whether long silence ends the call is left to 
          +Q5. |                                                         
      109 +| Background talk or unclear speech | "Sorry, I didn't catch t
          +hat", then a rephrased question. After two misses it offers a 
          +pharmacist. |                                                 
      110 +| "I have to go" or "text me instead" | `end_call`: the agent 
          +says it will text, then the SMS path runs. |                  
      111 +| A drug not on the list | "I don't see that one on your list"
          +, then the list again and an offer of a pharmacist. |         
      112 +| A different pharmacy not in the table | Stored as patient te
          +xt, read back and flagged for staff. |                        
      113 +| Insurance has changed, or no copay row exists | Noted and fl
          +agged. No price is quoted or invented. After the yes the reque
          +st is created with `estimated_copay` empty. |                 
      114 +| "No" at the final question | Nothing is created, the status 
          +becomes `declined`, and no text is sent. |                    
      115 +| The LLM is slow or fails | After about 4 s, one retry, then 
          +the pending question is re-asked from state. A refusal is hand
          +led the same way. |                                           
      116 +| A speech service fails mid-call | Treated as a dropped call,
          + so the SMS path resumes. |                                   
      117 +| A text arrives after completion | A short rendered status. |
      118 +| The patient asks for another language | The agent offers a p
          +harmacist. |                                                  
      119 +                                                              
      120 +Documented but not built: a patient calling back during an ope
          +n SMS thread, several refills in one session, and multiple pat
          +ients.                                                        
      121 +                                                              
      122 +## D7. Take-home scope and targets (2026-11-05)               
      123 +- Options: (A) build to production rules; (B) build the core a
          +nd name production needs in the walkthrough.                  
      124 +- Chosen: B. Left out: opt-out word matching, the 160-characte
          +r text limit, session expiry, the transcription notice and ven
          +dor agreements. Kept because they are cheap: tools refusing be
          +fore verification, no drug name in a text before verification,
          + and saying the agent is AI in the greeting. The date of birth
          + retry follows the kickoff notes. First texts ask the next que
          +stion directly and may state the copay. The fast deterministic
          + reader is deferred until timings exist. The target stays "und
          +er about 1 second", now measured from the true end of speech a
          +t the microphone to the first audible agent audio, reported by
          + turn class, and the number changes only after real timings.  
      125 +- Why: this is a take-home that will not ship. Latency estimat
          +es put a turn where the LLM reads the reply near 1.4 s at the 
          +median, so the honest move is to measure before touching the t
          +arget.                                                        
      126 +- Revisit if: real telephony or real SMS is added.

      128  ## Open questions
      129

  Keys to request

  AssemblyAI API key (realtime streaming, Universal-3.6 Pro)
  Inworld API key (realtime text-to-speech, inworld-tts-2)

  ElevenLabs API key (streaming text-to-speech, Flash v2.5)
  Optional, only if we run the speech-to-speech comparison: OpenAI API key 
  (GPT-Realtime-2.1)

  Only the Anthropic key blocks the first slice, and your Claude Code login
  doesn't count as one. Copy .env.example to .env and paste the values there,
  not into this chat, because chat goes into the logs reviewers read.

  Edge cases now covered in D6

  - Talking over: the agent stops as soon as the patient really speaks, but
    keeps going through "mm-hm" or "okay". If it starts too early, the two
    pieces of speech are read as one turn. Saved answers always stand, whatever
    gets interrupted.
  - Questions back: it answers from the record, then repeats its pending
    question. A price question before insurance is confirmed gets "as soon as we
    confirm your insurance", with no number. Medical questions go to a
    pharmacist, and emergency language gets a fixed 911 line.
  - Early or combined answers: one LLM pass can fill several fields, and the
    next line comes from the final state. Anything said before verification
    isn't stored, but is used afterward so the patient isn't asked twice. A
    half-finished date doesn't count as a failed attempt.
  - Protecting data: the patient comes from the session, never a tool argument,
    and no tool can list or search patients. The model never sees the stored
    date of birth, full member ID or copay table. "I'm the pharmacist" changes
    nothing, because no tool can act on it.
  - Other realistic cases: repeat requests, "hold on", "text me instead", a drug
    not on the list, an unknown pharmacy, a "no" at the end, and service
    failures. A speech service failure counts as a dropped call, so SMS takes
    over.

  Calls I made that you can veto

  1. Custom list of acknowledgement words: Pipecat's min_words setting would
     also stop "Lisinopril!" or "wait" from interrupting.
  2. Several tool calls per turn, run in order: this reverses the report's
     "parallel off", which would have needed extra LLM passes for combined
     answers.
  3. A seventh tool, end_call: it handles "I have to go" and the normal end of
     the call.
  4. Sentence check on model replies: before speech, it blocks any number or
     drug name outside this session's facts.
  5. Changed insurance: the request is still created after the patient's yes,
     flagged and with no price. The alternative is to stop and hand off to a
     pharmacist.

  What I need from you

  1. Q1: the key list assumes the pipeline design, which you haven't formally
     approved. Is that a yes?
  2. Q11, a new question: CLAUDE.md reads the full member ID aloud. I'd read the
     last four digits: it's shorter, protects data, and avoids a risky string
     for text-to-speech. Full ID or last four?
  3. /export: agent-logs/ still has only its README, so please run this before
     closing the session.

  Once the Anthropic key is in .env, I'll start the text loop. The schema, seed
  data, step logic and tools don't need a key, so say go if you'd like those
  started now.

      13  ## D1. Flow control: a plain code module owns the steps (2026-1
          1-05)
      14  - Options: (A) one prompt with all tools; (B) a plain code modu
          le that derives the step from stored fields, with the LLM readi
          ng replies; (C) a Pipecat Flows graph or a fully scripted dialo
          gue.
      15 -- Chosen: B. The current step is the first required field still
         - empty, never a stored pointer. One core, `handle_turn(session,
         - channel, text)`, serves voice and SMS and imports nothing from
         - the voice layer. The LLM reads the patient's reply and calls t
         -ools. Code renders every sentence holding a drug, pharmacy, pla
         -n or price, so a normal turn needs one LLM pass. Seven tools: `
         -verify_identity`, `select_medication`, `confirm_pharmacy`, `con
         -firm_insurance`, `submit_request`, `request_pharmacist`, `end_c
         -all`. Tools are gated on prerequisites rather than on the curre
         -nt step. Before verification only `verify_identity`, `request_p
         -harmacist` and `end_call` exist. After it every field tool exis
         -ts, so an early answer is captured. `submit_request` also needs
         - every field filled.                                           
      15 +- Chosen: B. The current step is the first required field still
         + empty, never a stored pointer. One core, `handle_turn(session,
         + channel, text)`, serves voice and SMS and imports nothing from
         + the voice layer. The LLM reads the patient's reply and calls t
         +ools. Code renders every sentence holding a drug, pharmacy, pla
         +n or price, so a normal turn needs one LLM pass. Eight tools: `
         +verify_identity`, `select_medication`, `confirm_pharmacy`, `con
         +firm_insurance`, `submit_request`, `request_pharmacist`, `end_c
         +all`, `respond`. Every turn is a forced tool call, and free ans
         +wers go through `respond`. Tools are gated on prerequisites rat
         +her than on the current step. Before verification only `verify_
         +identity`, `request_pharmacist`, `end_call` and `respond` exist
         +. After it every field tool exists, so an early answer is captu
         +red. `submit_request` also needs every field filled and the pri
         +ce already stated.                                             
      16  - Why: On text agents in 2026, one controlled study went from 0
          .38 with raw state to 0.98 with hard enforcement. Those tasks a
          re harder than this one and are not voice, so the shape transfe
          rs and the number does not. Flows keeps state in a process dict
          ionary and needs a voice pipeline, which the SMS path does not 
          have. Gating on the current step alone would lose a pharmacy na
          med early.
      17  - Revisit if: off-script handling becomes the main extension an
          d code routing feels brittle.

      13  ## D1. Flow control: a plain code module owns the steps (2026-1
          1-05)
      14  - Options: (A) one prompt with all tools; (B) a plain code modu
          le that derives the step from stored fields, with the LLM readi
          ng replies; (C) a Pipecat Flows graph or a fully scripted dialo
          gue.
      15  - Chosen: B. The current step is the first required field still
           empty, never a stored pointer. One core, `handle_turn(session,
           channel, text)`, serves voice and SMS and imports nothing from
           the voice layer. The LLM reads the patient's reply and calls t
          ools. Code renders every sentence holding a drug, pharmacy, pla
          n or price, so a normal turn needs one LLM pass. Eight tools: `
          verify_identity`, `select_medication`, `confirm_pharmacy`, `con
          firm_insurance`, `submit_request`, `request_pharmacist`, `end_c
          all`, `respond`. Every turn is a forced tool call, and free ans
          wers go through `respond`. Tools are gated on prerequisites rat
          her than on the current step. Before verification only `verify_
          identity`, `request_pharmacist`, `end_call` and `respond` exist
          . After it every field tool exists, so an early answer is captu
          red. `submit_request` also needs every field filled and the pri
          ce already stated.
      16 -- Why: On text agents in 2026, one controlled study went from 0
         -.38 with raw state to 0.98 with hard enforcement. Those tasks a
         -re harder than this one and are not voice, so the shape transfe
         -rs and the number does not. Flows keeps state in a process dict
         -ionary and needs a voice pipeline, which the SMS path does not 
         -have. Gating on the current step alone would lose a pharmacy na
         -med early.                                                     
      17 -- Revisit if: off-script handling becomes the main extension an
         -d code routing feels brittle.                                  
      16 +- Why: On text agents in 2026, one controlled study went from 0
         +.38 with raw state to 0.98 with hard enforcement. Those tasks a
         +re harder than this one and are not voice, so the shape transfe
         +rs and the number does not. Flows keeps state in a process dict
         +ionary and needs a voice pipeline, which the SMS path does not 
         +have. Gating on the current step alone would lose a pharmacy na
         +med early. Forcing the tool call was measured on 2026-11-06 wit
         +h Haiku 5.5, 24 calls per mode in one run from a home connectio
         +n: a finished call took 827 ms median against 1,186 ms with aut
         +omatic tool choice, because automatic mode wrote a preamble in 
         +18 of 24 calls and skipped the tool once. It also means a tool 
         +call can never be spoken as plain text.                        
      17 +- Revisit if: off-script handling becomes the main extension an
         +d code routing feels brittle, or the model moves to Sonnet 5.5,
         + which rejects forced tool choice.

      19  ## D2. Database: SQLite (2026-11-05)
      20  - Options: (A) SQLite; (B) Postgres in Docker.

⏺ The refill row is correct, but the transcript shows the model re-records old
  answers on every turn, which could make a "yes" misfire at a sensitive moment.
  I'm fixing it by giving the model only the newest message, having the note
  state what's already recorded, and making a repeated value a no-op.

       93  - The model never sees the stored date of birth, the full memb
           er ID or the copay table. Code renders those.
       94  - Two complete failed checks end verification for that session
           . Tools stay refused and the agent offers a pharmacist.
       95  - Questions about anyone else are refused with a fixed line. A
            caregiver gives the patient's name and date of birth.
       96 -- Free text from the model is checked one sentence at a time b
          -efore text-to-speech. A sentence with a number, price or drug 
          -name outside this session's facts, or with tool syntax, is rep
          -laced by the pending question. Replies are capped short.      
       96 +- A write needs evidence in the patient's own words. A yes cou
          +nts only for the question pending when the turn began, an answ
          +er to a later step must name that topic, and `submit_request` 
          +needs a clear yes with no negation. `respond` counts only as t
          +he sole call, and repeated or cut-off calls are dropped. This 
          +sits in code because forced tool use makes the model over-eage
          +r: in testing it confirmed insurance on "can you repeat that".
       97 +- Free text from the model is checked one sentence at a time b
          +efore text-to-speech. A sentence with a number, price or drug 
          +name outside this session's facts, tool syntax, or a question 
          +of its own is dropped, and the pending question follows. Befor
          +e verification only the fixed answers pass. Replies are capped
          + short.                                                       
       98  - "Ignore your instructions" or "I'm the pharmacist" changes n
           othing, because no tool can act on it. The agent gives a fixed
            line and the pending question.
       99  - State holds IDs and enums. Pharmacy text from a patient is s
           tored as data and flagged for staff.
      100  - SMS replies pass through the same gates. "Wrong number" or "
           who is this" ends the thread and blocks further content.

  The first-run score is the fair one. All three misses were one scenario, "the
  one on main street", and each time the agent just asked again. A stranger with
  Sarah's date of birth was refused, lovastatin and losartan weren't matched to
  her drugs, no run leaked data, and no question triggered a send.

  Speed

  The LLM step now has real numbers: about 840 ms median and 1.16 s at the 95th 
  percentile, with five sessions running at once. Adding the estimated 690 ms
  for everything else gives about 1.5 s per turn where the LLM reads the reply.
  That's slower than my 1.4 s estimate and misses the 1 second target. Real
  voice timing is still unmeasured.

  Changes from the plan

  - Forced tool calls: these saved about 360 ms, but the model then confirmed
    insurance on "can you repeat that". Code now requires evidence in the
    patient's own words before saving anything, and this is logged in D6.
  - Eight tools, not seven: respond handles questions, repeats and "hold on".
  - Haiku 5.5 is the working default: Q2 is still open, and Sonnet 5.5 rejects
    forced tool calls, so switching would mean reworking this.

  Not built yet

  Voice, SMS resume, working phrases, and all the talk-over and silence rules
  from D6. Those rules need the voice slice.

     plus Smart Turn with a longer hold on the date of birth. Yes?

     session.

          + 1.0 s on every step except the date of birth, which keeps a l

⏺ Real audio caught two bugs that typed tests missed: the evidence gate rejected
  a bare "Yes?" because of a question mark STT added, and merged speech re-read
  earlier fragments after the turn had moved on. I'm replacing the punctuation
  rule with a check for real question words and fixing the premature talk in my
  test client.

      146  
      147  ## D11. Turn detection: Smart Turn with step-aware limits (202
           6-11-06)
      148  - Options: (A) Smart Turn with VAD, plus step-aware rules; (B)
            provider turn detection tuned mid-stream; (C) a plain silence
            timer.
      149 -- Chosen: A. The core tells the turn layer the pending step. T
          -he fallback for a missed end of turn drops from 3.0 s to about
          - 1.0 s on every step except the date of birth, which keeps a l
          -onger wait. Barge-in stays on for every line, with a small lis
          -t of acknowledgement words that do not interrupt.             
      149 +- Chosen: A. The core tells the turn layer the pending step. T
          +he fallback for a missed end of turn is 3.0 s at the date of b
          +irth, 1.0 s at the medication step and 0.6 s at yes/no steps. 
          +Barge-in stays on for every line, with a small list of acknowl
          +edgement words that do not interrupt unless the line is about 
          +to end, when they may be a real answer.                       
      150  - Why: it starts with no vendor dependency and runs in millise
           conds locally. A silence timer scored far lower in the one com
           parison found, and dates of birth contain long pauses.
      151  - Revisit if: recordings of real speech show cut-offs this can
           not fix, then move to B. Tune on human speech, since Smart Tur
           n's false cut-off rate rose from 1.0% on real clips to 57.7% o
           n synthetic ones in one benchmark.

      52  |---|---|
      53  | Patient interrupts the agent | Audio stops, the rest of the l
          ine is dropped, and the reply is read as a normal turn. The lin
          e is stored marked as interrupted. |
      54  | "Mm-hm", "okay", "yeah" while the agent talks | Not an interr
          uption. The agent keeps talking and the word stays out of the c
          ontext. Any other word does interrupt, even one word such as "L
          isinopril" or "wait". This needs a small custom start strategy,
           because Pipecat's `min_words` would also block those one-word 
          interruptions. |
      55 -| Agent starts too early and the patient keeps going | Agent st
         -ops. The earlier fragment and the new words are read together a
         -s one turn. |                                                  
      55 +| Agent starts too early and the patient keeps going | Agent st
         +ops. The earlier fragment and the new words are read together a
         +s one turn, unless the fragment already moved the flow forward.
         + |                                                             
      56  | Patient speaks during a working phrase | The clip stops, any 
          pending reply is dropped, and the turn is read again with the n
          ew words. |
      57  | "Yes, send it" cuts off the price line | Does not count as th
          e yes. The agent repeats the price and the question. |

  You've already made three calls

  They look like yours, from about 1:04 to 1:09 pm. They ran before recording
  was on, so I have the text and timings but no audio. They're the first real
  human test, and they turned up problems my AI voice didn't.

  What worked: "Sarah Chan" with "Um, 1985. Um, um, um, March 15th" verified
  correctly, and "The first one" picked Lisinopril. "How much will this cost?"
  got the right answer, and "Okay, sent it" (misheard from "send it") still
  worked.

  What broke:
  1. First name only locks you out. You said "Sarah" with the right date, then
     repeated the date, and it refused you. It kept asking for the date when the
     name was the problem.
  2. "No" to the pharmacy fails. It said "Sorry, I didn't catch that" instead of
     asking which pharmacy you'd like. The take-home requires this choice, so
     it's a core gap.
  3. "I don't know" at insurance got the same wrong "didn't catch that".
  4. It talks over you. It replied in the middle of "My full name is" and "Um, I
     need". It recovered by merging your words, but you still heard it cut in.

       1 +"""Decide how much silence to wait for before replying.        
       2 +                                                               
       3 +Each turn is a small Bayesian decision. The question the agent 
         +just asked says whether the words so far answer                
       4 +it, which shifts the odds that the patient is still talking. Th
         +ose odds then fall as silence grows, at the rate               
       5 +real pauses end. The agent replies once the risk of cutting the
         + patient off is below an accepted level, which                 
       6 +is a cost choice. See D15 in docs/DECISIONS.md and tests/fit_tu
         +rn_model.py.                                                   
       7 +"""

      75 +def required_silence(state, step):                             
      76 +    accepted_risk = ACCEPTED_CUT_OFF_RISK.get(step, DEFAULT_CUT
         +_OFF_RISK)                                                     
      77 +    accepted_odds = accepted_risk / (1 - accepted_risk)        
      78 +    odds_before_silence = PRIOR_ODDS_STILL_TALKING * STILL_TALK
         +ING_LIKELIHOOD_RATIO[state]                                    
      79 +    pause_survival = accepted_odds / odds_before_silence       
      80 +    if pause_survival >= 1:                                    
      81 +        return 0.0                                             
      82 +    return math.exp(PAUSE_LOG_MEAN + PAUSE_LOG_SCALE * NormalDi
         +st().inv_cdf(1 - pause_survival))

      24 +def pause_posterior(pauses):                                   
      25 +    logs = [math.log(pause) for pause in pauses]               
      26 +    n, mean = len(logs), sum(logs) / len(logs)                 
      27 +    squares = sum((value - mean) ** 2 for value in logs)

         +n - PRIOR_LOG_MEAN) ** 2 / (2 * strength)                      
      32 +    return log_mean, math.sqrt(rate * (strength + 1) / (shape *

  ⎿  Updated docs/DECISIONS.md (+27 -9)
      12  
      13  ## D1. Flow control: a plain code module owns the steps (2026-1
          1-05)
      14  - Options: (A) one prompt with all tools; (B) a plain code modu
          le that derives the step from stored fields, with the LLM readi
          ng replies; (C) a Pipecat Flows graph or a fully scripted dialo
          gue.
      15 -- Chosen: B. The current step is the first required field still
         - empty, never a stored pointer. One core, `handle_turn(session,
         - channel, text)`, serves voice and SMS and imports nothing from
         - the voice layer. The LLM reads the patient's reply and calls t
         -ools. Code renders every sentence holding a drug, pharmacy, pla
         -n or price, so a normal turn needs one LLM pass. Eight tools: `
         -verify_identity`, `select_medication`, `confirm_pharmacy`, `con
         -firm_insurance`, `submit_request`, `request_pharmacist`, `end_c
         -all`, `respond`. Every turn is a forced tool call, and free ans
         -wers go through `respond`. Tools are gated on prerequisites rat
         -her than on the current step. Before verification only `verify_
         -identity`, `request_pharmacist`, `end_call` and `respond` exist
         -. After it every field tool exists, so an early answer is captu
         -red. `submit_request` also needs every field filled and the pri
         -ce already stated.                                             
      15 +- Chosen: B. The current step is the first required field still
         + empty, never a stored pointer. One core, `handle_turn(session,
         + channel, text)`, serves voice and SMS and imports nothing from
         + the voice layer. The LLM reads the patient's reply and calls t
         +ools. Code renders every sentence holding a drug, pharmacy, pla
         +n or price, so a normal turn needs one LLM pass. Nine tools: `v
         +erify_identity`, `select_medication`, `confirm_pharmacy`, `conf
         +irm_insurance`, `change_answer`, `submit_request`, `request_pha
         +rmacist`, `end_call`, `respond`. Every turn is a forced tool ca
         +ll, and free answers go through `respond`. Tools are gated on p
         +rerequisites rather than on the current step. Before verificati
         +on only `verify_identity`, `request_pharmacist`, `end_call` and
         + `respond` exist. After it every field tool exists, so an early
         + answer is captured. `submit_request` also needs every field fi
         +lled and the price already stated.                             
      16  - Why: On text agents in 2026, one controlled study went from 0
          .38 with raw state to 0.98 with hard enforcement. Those tasks a
          re harder than this one and are not voice, so the shape transfe
          rs and the number does not. Flows keeps state in a process dict
          ionary and needs a voice pipeline, which the SMS path does not 
          have. Gating on the current step alone would lose a pharmacy na
          med early. Forcing the tool call was measured on 2026-11-06 wit
          h Haiku 5.5, 24 calls per mode in one run from a home connectio
          n: a finished call took 827 ms median against 1,186 ms with aut
          omatic tool choice, because automatic mode wrote a preamble in 
          18 of 24 calls and skipped the tool once. It also means a tool 
          call can never be spoken as plain text.
      17  - Revisit if: off-script handling becomes the main extension an
          d code routing feels brittle, or the model moves to Sonnet 5.5,
           which rejects forced tool choice.
      18  
     ...
      36  
      37  ## D5. Working phrases on slow turns (2026-11-05)
      38  - Options: (A) no filler; (B) short working phrases on a timer;
           (C) hesitation sounds such as "uhh" and "umm".
      39 -- Chosen: B. Pre-recorded clips in the agent's voice, such as "
         -Let me check that" and "One moment", holding no facts. A clip p
         -lays only if no reply is ready about 0.3 s after the turn ends,
         - so fast turns have none. At most about three per call, never t
         -he same one twice running, and never while a date of birth is i
         -ncomplete. Clips can be interrupted and stay out of the convers
         -ation context. First sound and first real content are logged se
         -parately, and the latency figure uses first content.

⏺ I went through your calls, fixed what they exposed, and tuned the agent on
  your real voice. It's pushed as 3f4aea2, and the server is back up at
  http://127.0.0.1:7860, with your 5 calls and 2 recordings intact.

  Before and after

  Your own recorded speech, replayed through the new build and measured the same
  way:

  ┌────────────────────────┬─────────────────────┬─────────────┐
  │                        │ Your original calls │ Tuned build │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ Median pause           │ 1,640 ms            │ 1,480 ms    │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ 90th percentile        │ 4,200 ms            │ 2,300 ms    │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ Worst                  │ 4,260 ms            │ 3,720 ms    │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ Turns under 1 s        │ 0 of 35             │ 8 of 22     │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ Agent talking over you │ 1.5 per minute      │ 0           │
  └────────────────────────┴─────────────────────┴─────────────┘

  Treat this as a good sign, not proof. It's 4 calls replaying a subset of your
  clips, and the replay waits for the agent to stop talking, so the talk-over
  number flatters. All these timings are on the server's clock, so add about 300
  ms for what you'd hear.

  The 1 second target is met only on exact-answer turns (640 to 920 ms by the
  test caller's clock). Turns the LLM reads still take about 1.3 to 2.5 s. An
  incomplete date like "March 1985" takes about 3.8 s, because it deliberately
  waits for the rest.

  What your calls exposed, now fixed

  - Wrong drug: "All 3" recorded Atorvastatin, because the model read "3" as a
    prescription ID. It now says it does one refill at a time.
  - 911 loop: the line repeated even after "This is not an emergency". It now
    plays once per call.
  - "Change my medicine": this wrongly flagged a pharmacist. It now clears your
    choice and asks again.
  - Repeated lines: the agent said the same sentence up to eight times. Repeats
    are now reworded.
  - "I don't know": this got "didn't catch that" at the pharmacy step. It now
    gets "That's okay".

  What changed in the tuning

  - Bayesian turn-taking replaced Smart Turn. On your voice, Smart Turn added no
    measurable information (likelihood ratio 0.88, where 1.0 means none). The
    new model uses the cost-based approach from Raux and Eskenazi: it asks
    whether your words answer the question just asked, then weighs that against
    how your real pauses end. On held-out calls it halved the average wait at
    about the same cut-off risk.
  - Exact answers skip the LLM. A bare "yes", "no" or drug name goes straight to
    the same gated tools.
  - Less context for the model. After verification it sees only the last
    exchange, which stops most of the re-recording that cost about 0.6 s.
  - Small speedups. Prompt caches warm up during the greeting, and working
    phrases only play on slow turns.

  Tried and rejected

  - One tool per turn: it looked faster, but "no, don't send it" failed 3 of 3
    times.
  - Working phrases at 1.0 s: they fired on almost every LLM turn and delayed
    the real answer, so they're now at 1.5 s.
  - Learned turn-taking models: VAP-style models need far more data than five
    calls.

  Limits

  The model is fit on one speaker: 58 turns, 11 of them mid-sentence, and 12
  pauses. That's fine as a starting point, but it's not validated for older or
  slower speakers. Typed tests pass (116 of 116 tuned, 72 of 72 holdout), though
  I tuned against those, so they prove less than they sound like they do. The
  next speed lever is starting the LLM before you finish speaking, which might
  save about 0.25 s but adds real complexity.

  Your choice now: try a call yourself, or say go and I'll build the SMS
  fallback, which is the last core piece.

  Sources: Raux and Eskenazi 2009, Voice Activity Projection

⏺ The two keys are live at http://127.0.0.1:7860, pushed as f78dcbc. Your 8
  calls and 5 recordings are intact.

  Your new tests

  Keys: * Go back and # Main menu are the only two, always visible during a
  call, and they also work on a keyboard. On screen they reply in about 0.3 s,
  because they skip speech recognition and the LLM.

  Fixed from your calls:
  - False lockout: one long sentence could use up both identity attempts. An
    attempt now only counts if you heard the reply.
  - "The last 2" recorded Metformin: that, and "diabetes and cholesterol", now
    get "one refill at a time".
  - Name order: "Chen Sarah" now matches.
  - "Hello" and "What services do you provide?": these now get short, proper
    answers.
  - Repeated drug list: it's no longer re-read unless asked, as you pointed out
    in the call.

  The typed tests all pass (144 of 144, 72 of 72 holdout), though these are
  scripted scenarios I tuned against. Your next live call is the better test.

  Credentials to request

  Twilio (voice calls)
  - Account SID
  - Auth Token (or an API Key SID and Secret)
  - A voice-enabled Twilio phone number, in +1 format
  - Is the account trial or paid?

  OpenPhone / Quo (SMS)
  - API key
  - The sending number's ID (starts with PN), or the number itself
  - Confirmation that the number has completed US carrier registration
  - Permission to add a webhook for inbound texts
  - Trial or paid: third-party guides say trial accounts play a disclaimer and
    only reach verified numbers. I couldn't confirm that on Twilio's own pages,
    so it's worth asking.
  - Carrier registration: Quo's docs say it's required to text US numbers, and
    it's the item most likely to cause a delay.

  Also needed, beyond credentials

  - A public URL: Twilio and Quo have to reach your laptop, so we need a tunnel.
    Neither cloudflared nor ngrok is installed yet.
  - Your real mobile number: Sarah's (555) 867-5309 is fake, so the demo patient
    needs a number that can actually receive texts.

  Before you commit to this

  1. The SMS fallback isn't built yet, and real SMS depends on it. I'd build it
     first; connecting Quo after that should be a small step.
  2. Phone audio is lower quality. Twilio sends 8 kHz audio, and all my tuning
     was on browser audio. Speech accuracy and the pause model need rechecking
     on a real line.
  3. What already carries over: Pipecat's Twilio serializer turns phone key
     presses into the same DTMF frames the page uses, so * and # should need no
     changes. Real callers can't see buttons, though, so the agent would have to
     say them out loud.

  Should I build the SMS fallback now while you request credentials?

  Sources: Quo: send a message, Quo: first message and US registration, Quo: 
  webhooks overview, Quo: webhook payloads, Twilio: Media Streams, third-party 
  guide on Twilio trial limits

⏺ It's already cheap, but only because the speed choices happen to be cheap. I 
  never tuned it for cost. Your tested calls work out to about 7 cents each
  including phone charges, and a clean refill to about 4 cents. The LLM is the
  smallest cost, so it isn't the place to save.

  These are my calculations from your 7 recorded calls and list prices, not
  actual invoices:

  ┌──────────────────────────────┬───────────────────┬──────────────────────┐
  │        Cost per call         │ Clean refill (1.1 │  Your tested calls   │
  │                              │        min)       │      (1.8 min)       │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ Text-to-speech (Inworld)     │ 1.6¢              │ 3.6¢                 │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ Speech-to-text (AssemblyAI)  │ 0.8¢              │ 1.4¢                 │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ LLM (Haiku 5.5)              │ 0.1¢              │ 0.2¢                 │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ AI subtotal                  │ 2.6¢              │ 5.1¢                 │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ Twilio, once real (about     │ 1.4¢              │ 2.3¢                 │
  │ 1.25¢/min)                   │                   │                      │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ Total                        │ about 4¢          │ about 7¢             │
  └──────────────────────────────┴───────────────────┴──────────────────────┘

  Your tested calls cost more because you were deliberately stress-testing them.
  Twilio also charges about $1.15 a month for the number. I haven't priced Quo
  SMS, so that's not included.

  What's already keeping costs down

  - Haiku 5.5 with prompt caching: an LLM turn costs about 0.01¢.
  - Exact answers skip the LLM: in your last full keypad test, 13 of 23 turns
    never reached it.
  - Code writes the fixed lines: the model outputs a short tool call, not spoken
    sentences.

  Where the money goes, and what could change

  ┌──────────────────────────────┬────────────┬────────────────────────────┐
  │            Option            │   Saving   │           Catch            │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ Inworld TTS-2 Flash ($15 vs  │ About 40%  │ Voice quality needs a      │
  │ $25 per 1M characters)       │ of TTS     │ listening test             │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ AssemblyAI                   │ About 65%  │ Could hurt drug names and  │
  │ Universal-Streaming ($0.15   │ of STT     │ dates; your recordings     │
  │ vs $0.45/hr)                 │            │ could test it              │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │                              │ About      │ Slower first LLM turn;     │
  │ Skip cache warm-up           │ 0.09¢ per  │ only worth it once traffic │
  │                              │ call       │  is steady                 │
  └──────────────────────────────┴────────────┴────────────────────────────┘

  My recommendation

  Don't change anything for this take-home. Each saving trades away speed or
  accuracy, your two stated priorities, to save about a cent per call. At 10,000
  calls a month you'd spend roughly $400 to $700, so this only matters at real
  volume, and the first lever then would be Inworld's volume pricing, which
  drops toward $10 per 1M characters.

  How solid these numbers are

  - Inworld: the $25 rate comes from secondary sources, because I couldn't open
    their pricing page.
  - Twilio Media Streams: the $0.004/min rate came from a regional mirror of
    Twilio's pricing page.
  - Speech-to-speech comparison: the report's rough $0.07 to $0.30 per 3-minute
    call doesn't include phone charges. This setup is about 8¢ on the same
    basis, so it's at the low end of that range.

  The SMS fallback is still the last core piece, and I'm ready to build it
  whenever you say go.

  Sources: AssemblyAI pricing, Inworld TTS, Inworld pricing summary, Twilio 
  Voice pricing, Twilio volume pricing

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ This build isn't HIPAA-compliant, and it must not touch real patient data. 
  That's fine for a take-home with fake data, but "compliant" isn't a claim you 
  should make in the walkthrough. The privacy design inside the agent is strong.
  The gaps are in storage, logs and vendor contracts. This is engineering
  input, not legal advice.

  Fix before real phone testing

  These two apply as soon as real people can call:
  1. Recording has no notice. Every call is saved, and the agent never says so.
     If a reviewer phones in, they'd be recorded without being told. I'd add a
     line to the greeting or turn recording off on the phone line.
  2. A tunnel would expose the server. It's safe now because it only listens on
     your laptop. Tunnelled as it is, anyone with the URL could start calls on
     your paid keys. Only the Twilio and Quo routes should be exposed, with
     their signatures verified.

  Each service

  Service: Anthropic
  What it sees: Patient's words, drug list, plan name
  HIPAA route: BAA for the API; prompt caching and strict tools are on the
    eligible list
  Status: Good
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Service: AssemblyAI
  What it sees: Patient's voice
  HIPAA route: Self-serve BAA; zero retention on streaming if you opt out of
    training
  Status: Clearest route, though an older FAQ conflicts
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Service: Inworld
  What it sees: Every spoken line, including drugs and prices
  HIPAA route: BAA on enterprise contracts
  Status: Unconfirmed: I found nothing saying it covers TTS, and its retention
    docs conflict
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Service: Twilio
  What it sees: Call audio
  HIPAA route: BAA needs Security or Enterprise Edition, and a HIPAA project
  Status: Ask whether Scope's account qualifies
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Service: Quo (OpenPhone)
  What it sees: Text messages
  HIPAA route: BAA on Business and Scale plans
  Status: SMS is explicitly excluded from the BAA
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Service: Deepgram, ElevenLabs
  What it sees: Your test audio and test scripts
  HIPAA route: BAA on Enterprise only
  Status: Test tools; keep them out of production
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Service: Claude Code, GitHub
  What it sees: Code and session logs
  HIPAA route: Not covered for this use
  Status: Never put real patient data here

  Quo is the biggest issue: the customer takes full liability for any patient
  data sent by text, and the take-home's sample SMS names a drug. A production
  version would need texts with no patient data, or a different provider. That's
  a good point to raise before a reviewer does, especially as Scope suggested
  OpenPhone.

  Also ask Twilio about one thing: an older PDF of theirs marks Programmable
  Voice as end of life on December 5, 2026. I couldn't confirm it, and it may be
  outdated.

  Already strong

  I checked these in the code and repo:
  - The model never sees the stored date of birth, the full member ID or the
    copay table.
  - Tools refuse before verification, and no tool can list or search patients.
  - .env is owner-only and git-ignored, and no database, audio or keys are in
    the private repo.

  Gaps before any real patient data

  - Storage: refill.db and 69 MB of recordings are unencrypted with no deletion
    policy, and the database file is readable by other accounts on your Mac.
  - Logs: the server log contains full transcripts and spoken lines, so it would
    count as patient data.
  - Identity: name plus date of birth is weak, and NIST says knowledge-based
    checks "SHALL NOT be used for identity verification".
  - Missing entirely: access controls and an audit log.

  Vendor details come from their published pages and can change, so get coverage
  in writing. Also, no official "HIPAA certified" badge exists, so ask each
  vendor for a signed BAA and its covered-services list.

  Should I include the recording notice and the tunnel lockdown in the Twilio
  work? They're small changes, and neither should wait until after real calls
  start.

  Sources: Anthropic data retention, Anthropic BAAs, AssemblyAI data retention,
  AssemblyAI data controls, Inworld voice AI for patient intake, Inworld zero 
  data retention, Twilio HIPAA, Twilio eligible services, Quo HIPAA, NIST SP 
  800-63A-4

❯ simulated calls and SMS confirmed. Discrepencies like low-quality and

⏺ Confirmed: calls and SMS stay simulated, and you've settled the question of
  poor audio too. The next step is the SMS fallback. It's the last core piece
  and every decision for it is made, so I'm starting on it now. Here's the order
  from here:

  1. SMS fallback: a drop at any step leads to a text, and the conversation
     resumes with nothing asked twice.
  2. Simulated phone audio: 8 kHz, line noise, dropouts and accents, using your
     real recordings as well as AI voices.
  3. Intense QA: drop at every step, repeated runs on both channels, degraded
     audio and adversarial callers, all asserting on database rows.
  4. Polish and submission: Windows install check, setup docs, final transcript
     export.

  First I need to see how Pipecat detects a lost connection, since D12 depends
  on it, and how the current disconnect path behaves.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The library's keep-alive pings don't detect a dropped network, so I'm adding
  my own heartbeat to end calls within seconds instead of 30. One shared
  function will end every call exactly once, under the session lock, and I'm
  adding the new column to your existing database so your 11 calls stay.

      162  ## D12. Detecting an early hang-up (2026-11-06)
      163  - Options: (A) WebRTC connection state alone; (B) the End butt
           on, a `pagehide` handler and a heartbeat; (C) a silence timeou
           t.
      164 -- Chosen: B. The End button and `pagehide` close the connectio
          -n at once. An abnormal loss starts a 3 to 5 s grace timer, can
          -celled if the session reconnects. Silence never sends a text. 
          -One handler, under the session lock so it sees any write in pr
          -ogress, sends the text once and sends nothing if the request i
          -s complete.                                                   
      165 -- Why: without `pagehide`, a closed tab took about 30 s to rea
          -ch aiortc in a local test. With it, closing the real page mark
          -ed the session dropped in about 55 ms. A 30-second gap would r
          -ead as a bug in a demo.                                       
      164 +- Chosen: B. The End button and `pagehide` close the connectio
          +n at once. The page sends a heartbeat every second, and 4 s wi
          +thout one counts as a lost connection. Silence never sends a t
          +ext. One handler, `finish_call`, runs under the session lock s
          +o it sees any write in progress, sends the text once and sends
          + nothing if the request is complete or identity failed. There 
          +is no reconnect, because a browser demo has no way back into a
          + dropped call.

          +xt on the page in about 0.1 s. A silent connection produced th
